A node-targeted channel was rejected because reordering is not a property of any single section -- it is a property of the page | 7.5 |
| 2026-07-30 | A reorder must list EVERY section on its route: partial lists, unknown ids and duplicates are all hard export failures | Treating a partial list as "these first, the rest after" | The lenient reading is where silent content loss comes from -- a section omitted from the list would simply vanish from the exported page, and the omission would look like a reorder rather than a deletion. Deletion has its own channel (visibility), which tombstones the manifest entry so the loss is recorded. Since a reorder is generated by the editor from the full current list, a partial one always indicates a bug rather than an intent | 7.5 |
| 2026-07-30 | Reorder rewrites the JSX children of the page's fragment (PRD 3.3: "an index.tsx-level override ... NOT a DOM operation"), runs LAST of all channels, and leaves children with no `nodeId` where they are | Reordering by index alone | Running last matters because a visibility override REMOVES a section's JSX; reordering first would then be operating on a list that no longer matches. Preserving id-less children in place matters because `FailedSectionPlaceholder` deliberately carries no node id (pipeline 5.4) -- matching purely by index would have shuffled or dropped it. Verified with a real export including the production build, not just skipBuild: the rewritten fragment typechecks and builds | 7.5 |
| 2026-08-02 | **The live preview reorders the REAL DOM; it does NOT use flex `order`** | Making the page container `display:flex; flex-direction:column` and giving each section an `order` index -- no DOM mutation, nothing for React to fight, trivially idempotent | `order` only applies to flex/grid items, so faking the order requires changing the container's formatting context -- which switches OFF the vertical margin collapsing a block container does. The export reorders JSX and stays a block container, so preview and handover would lay the same sections out differently: a preview = handover violation, in the one area the whole architecture exists to protect. Worse, the invariant suite could not have caught it -- it frames each node by its own box, so a shifted position is invisible while the box looks identical. Moving real DOM nodes also makes order-dependent CSS (`:first-child`, `nth-child`, sibling combinators) resolve against the same DOM order on both sides. The cost is that React can restore its own order on re-render, which is the fight `applyTextOverrides` already has and is resolved the same way: the mutation observer re-runs the apply. It writes only when the order is actually wrong, so re-application terminates instead of feeding itself | 7.5 |
| 2026-08-02 | The reorder invariant case runs on the **`about`** route -- which gained a second fixture section (`AboutValues`) to make that possible -- and is the first case exempt from the pixel diff (`skipPixelDiff`) | Reordering `home`, which already has six sections and needed no fixture change | Every `home` section also carries a visibility case, and a ghosted node keeps its layout space in preview (PRD 3.4) while the export compiles it out (contract 6.2). So on `home` every node below a ghost ALREADY sits at a different absolute Y on the two sides -- correct and intended, and invisible to a per-node screenshot only while that offset stays a whole number of pixels. Reordering `home` changed those offsets and flipped two unrelated cases onto different pixel boundaries (measured: a 1.27% text-rasterization diff on a faq item, then a 1px crop difference on a testimonial quote) -- noise that says nothing about reorder, and tuning it case by case would have been whack-a-mole. `about` carries no visibility case, so both sections sit at identical positions on both sides. The exemption costs nothing because reorder changes only POSITION, never appearance, which a per-node screenshot cannot see anyway; the real assertion is a new section-order test that compares the section sequence in preview against export AND against an explicitly expected order, so it cannot pass on two identically-wrong orders (confirmed by arming it with the wrong order and watching it fail). Placing the new section AFTER the page's `FailedSectionPlaceholder` also made this the only end-to-end coverage of the id-less-child-holds-its-slot rule, which the exporter and the shim implement separately | 7.5 |
| 2026-08-02 | The faq visibility case now hides an answer inside the LAST list item rather than the middle one | Leaving it where it was, since it was passing | It was passing by luck of accumulated offsets. Hiding inside a middle item means every sibling below it sits higher in export than in preview, leaving those cases one rounding away from a spurious +/-1px failure -- which is exactly how the reorder work surfaced it. Hiding inside the last item leaves nothing below to shift, so the fragility does not exist rather than being tolerated. The visibility channel is still asserted identically, by absence from the export | 7.5 |
| 2026-08-02 | A large VERTICAL drag of a section is now the gesture that triggers a reorder, instead of being rejected as implied reordering | Leaving reorder to the inspector buttons only, and keeping the drag a rejection | The rejected-gesture log exists (PRD risk 3.3) precisely to show which refused gestures are worth supporting, and this is the first one the product grew the ability to express -- a drag past a sibling boundary always LOOKED like a reorder, and now it is one. Kept narrow on purpose: only a `move` gesture, only when the drag is more vertical than horizontal, and only one step per gesture, so a diagonal fling is still a rejection rather than a surprise move | 7.5 |
| 2026-08-02 | Page-level regeneration is a **sequential loop over `regenerate_section`**, not a page-level Kitaru replay and not a fan-out | Forking one page-level execution; or regenerating the route's sections in parallel the way first-generation fan-out does | There is no page-level execution to replay -- 7.1 established that recorded exec ids are per-section, so a page regen is inherently N forks. Looping the existing function (rather than writing a page path beside it) means page scope inherits the replay-with-overrides fork, gate 7 ID survival and orphan declaration unchanged, with no second code path that can drift. Sequential rather than parallel because every section on a route writes into the same page directory and the same manifest, which contract section 2 gives exactly one writer; the manifest service's cross-process lock would serialize the writes anyway, but only after two forks had already raced to produce them. Sequential also means a mid-page failure leaves a coherent prefix instead of an arbitrary subset | 7.9 |
| 2026-08-02 | The page path snapshots the route **once, before any section runs**; revert then needed no change at all | Snapshotting per section, reusing the existing per-section call inside the loop | The snapshot was always route-wide (`src/pages/<route>/` + `manifest.json`), so one revert restoring a whole page came free -- but only if the backup is taken before the first section. Snapshotting per section would leave the backup holding the PREVIOUS section's freshly regenerated output, so revert would silently undo only the last section while appearing to undo the page. The e2e test asserts a return to the state immediately before the regen (captured, not assumed -- earlier tests in that file leave the fixture project regenerated), which is what would catch it | 7.9 |
| 2026-08-02 | Orphaned overrides, tombstones and overridden ids are MERGED and deduplicated into one page-level outcome | Returning per-section outcomes and letting the editor show the orphan dialog once per section | PRD 4.3's orphan dialog is a decision point -- keep or discard this edit -- and asking it once per section turns one decision into a wall of prompts for exactly the operation most likely to orphan several at once. Failures are the deliberate exception: they stay per-section (`perSection`, and a failure report prefixed with the section id) on the same principle as the DAG report, that a failure must remain visible at the level above rather than being averaged away by passing siblings | 7.9 |
| 2026-08-02 | Mock regen now reads each section's component file from the MANIFEST instead of hardcoding `Hero` | Leaving the hardcoded path, since mock mode is only test infrastructure | A page regen loops every section, and the hardcoded path would have rewritten `Hero.data.ts` six times while reporting six regenerated sections -- a mock that looks like it works while touching one file is worse than no mock, because the e2e test built on it would pass regardless. The manifest is the node registry (contract section 2) and is what the real path reads too. Sections whose data has no `headline` are now left alone rather than reported as changed | 7.9 |
| 2026-08-02 | 7.9 was NOT verified with a live model run; the section path it loops was verified live in 7.1 | Spending a full page regen (~6 sections of real generation) to confirm the loop | The page path adds a loop, an aggregation and a snapshot ordering -- all covered directly by unit tests and the mock-mode e2e -- around a function already proven live. The one risk a live run would uniquely surface is sequential replays interfering on the same `run_id`; inspected instead, and they do not: each pass appends its own events, and `recorded_exec_id` deliberately takes the LATEST execution of the requested section, which is the one whose output is on disk. Recorded here so the gap is explicit rather than implied | 7.9 |
| 2026-08-02 | Add-a-section is a FIRST generation of one section into an existing project, not a Kitaru replay | Forking a recorded execution the way regen does, since PRD 4.1 calls it regen-style | There is nothing to fork: this section has never existed, so no execution recorded it. Regen-style describes the UX (pick, instruct, one section's cost), not the mechanism. It reuses `generate_section_flow` with `reuse_workspace=True` -- the same call page fan-out makes per section -- so the project's own generated tokens and primitives are the design context rather than the fixture's | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | The new section is APPENDED to the page's index.tsx and positioned by a `sectionOrder` override; the orchestrator never places it | Inserting the render line at the requested index in index.tsx | Position is not a property of source order once reorder exists -- an override already decides what the user sees, so a source insert would be immediately overridden anyway and the two could disagree. Keeping placement in the editor means the orchestrator writes source and the editor writes overrides, exactly as the ownership map requires, and nothing is renumbered: ids stay semantic and immutable (contract 5.2). This is why 7.6 was sequenced after 7.5 | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | index.tsx is APPENDED to textually, never re-assembled by `assemble_page_index_source` | Rebuilding the page from the manifest, which is the deterministic path first generation uses | `assemble_page_index_source` rebuilds from a section list that records which sections FAILED -- information the manifest does not hold, because a failed section never proposed a manifest entry. Re-assembling from the manifest would therefore silently delete the page's `FailedSectionPlaceholder` and shorten the page. Appending adds two imports and one render line and cannot lose anything; a test asserts the placeholder survives | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | A colliding section slug suffixes the NEW section (`pricing-tiers-2`), never the existing one | Rejecting the add, or renaming the existing section | Ids are immutable once registered (contract 5.2) and overrides bind to them, so renaming the existing section would orphan every edit on it. Rejecting would block a legitimate action -- two pricing sections on one page is a real thing to want. Collisions are scoped per route, since ids are route-prefixed and the same slug on two routes is not a collision at all | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | The archetype catalog is served to the editor from the orchestrator's own `ARCHETYPE_CATALOG` (`python -m orchestrator.catalog`), not duplicated in TypeScript | A TS copy of the archetype list in the editor, avoiding a subprocess | That table decides which archetypes have prompt templates, so a copy would offer the user a section the generator cannot build the moment the two drift. Exposed as a module entry point rather than `python -c`: the preview server spawns through a shell, which does not quote arguments on Windows, so a `-c` expression containing spaces and a semicolon is split into pieces -- which is exactly how it first failed | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | Archetype previews are SCHEMATIC block shapes, not screenshots | A rendered screenshot per archetype; or names and descriptions with no preview at all | PRD 4.1 asks for previews, and there is no honest screenshot to show: every instance is generated fresh, so a stock image would be someone else's output presented as what the user is about to get. A schematic shows the one thing the choice actually determines -- the SHAPE -- leaving content to the instruction. Unknown archetypes fall back to a generic shape, so the picker can never hide a buildable archetype just because the shape map is behind the catalog | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | The + affordance is a small centred button per boundary, faintly visible, NOT a full-width invisible strip | A transparent full-width hover strip at each boundary, which is the more discoverable pattern | The full-width version shipped first and the invariant suite caught it immediately: with `pointer-events: auto` it swallowed a corner-click on the hero section, and it would have swallowed real user clicks on section content near every boundary. A faint always-visible circle is also more discoverable than an invisible strip, which the user has to already know is there | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | 7.6 was NOT verified with a live model run | Spending one real section generation to confirm the flow end to end | The added section's generation IS `generate_section_flow`, which every soak, stress and acceptance run has exercised; what 7.6 adds is the wiring around it, covered by unit tests (slug allocation, index append, plan append) and a mock-mode e2e that ends in a real EXPORT -- typecheck plus all seven gates (contract section 8) -- so the added section is proven to be real, compiling code whose `sectionOrder` override the exporter accepts. Recorded so the gap is explicit rather than implied | 7.6 |
| 2026-08-02 | An end-to-end run now has a SECOND half: `orchestrator.acceptance_edit` exercises the editing loop (seed overrides across all 5 channels -> section regen -> page regen -> add-a-section -> re-export) against a real generated project | Leaving `acceptance.py` as the end-to-end test, since it already chains plan through export | `acceptance.py` proves a site can be GENERATED and stops there, so nothing in it touched what the product is actually for: editing a site and regenerating parts of it without losing the edits. That left the most important property in the whole system -- preview = handover across regeneration -- outside the only end-to-end runner. It also closes the two live-verification gaps 7.9 and 7.6 recorded, in the same run rather than as separate spends | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The harness asserts "no override was lost while its node survived", NOT "zero orphans" | Asserting zero orphaned overrides, which reads as the stronger check | Zero-orphans is the WRONG assertion in both directions: it fails a regeneration that behaved correctly (an override on an element the model legitimately dropped IS an orphan, and PRD 4.3 exists to surface exactly that), while still passing a run that silently dropped an edit whose node happens to remain. The check therefore splits the two cases: a node gone AND declared is reported, a node gone and NOT declared is a hard failure, and -- the inverse inconsistency, equally fatal -- a node still active yet reported orphaned is also a hard failure | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Override targets are derived from the generated project's own manifest `editable` lists, not hardcoded | Reusing `stress.install_overrides`, which writes a fixed set of `home.hero.*` ids | Those ids only exist in the fixture; every generated site has different ones, so a fixed list would silently seed zero valid overrides and the run would pass having tested nothing. Reading `editable` also means the harness can never seed a channel a node does not declare, which the exporter rejects -- a failure that would otherwise only appear at the very end of a paid run | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The whole harness was dry-run against the FIXTURE (copied into `generated/`, seeded, exported with gates + typecheck + production build) before any live spend | Going straight to a live run, since the harness is only test code | The risky part of a paid run is not the model, it is discovering after several dollars that a seeded override had the wrong value shape or that the reorder omitted a section. The fixture run costs nothing and exercises every non-model step: 5 overrides across all channels compiled and the export completed clean, including the production build | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | `compiler`'s vitest `testTimeout` raised to 120s | Leaving the 5s default, or serializing test files with `fileParallelism: false` | Much of that suite is not unit tests: exporter and gate tests copy the fixture and shell out to the project's own `tsc --noEmit` (gate 1). The zip-determinism test runs in 1.6s alone but was measured at 66s under parallel-file contention, so the default flakes on a loaded CI runner and nowhere else. Serializing files would have fixed it by making every run slower; a calibrated timeout costs nothing on a quiet machine and still fails a genuinely hung test | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The stale "full 15-primitive set" wording in build-plan and build-prompts was corrected (to name contract 4.1's minimum set plus 7.4's `Notice`) | Continuing to leave it flagged for a human | Flagged twice and then explicitly handed back with "finish pending items", which is the human call the standing rule asks for. The edit is a factual count in a descriptive narrative line, not a spec change made to let code pass -- the binding contract (4.1, "Minimum v1 set") was never violated | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | A new **app archetype set**: page archetype `app-screen` plus 8 section archetypes (app-shell, element-palette, builder-canvas, properties-inspector, form-renderer, data-toolbar, data-grid, detail-drawer), each with a dedicated template | Letting an app brief fall through to `generic-section`; or declining app briefs as out of scope | Spec 4.2 states outright that "growing this catalog is the main ongoing content work of the product", and 4.3 defines a page archetype as nothing more than a Planner-side prior -- so both additions are the sanctioned mechanism rather than a contract change. The fallback was not an option: 4.4 caps `custom` at ONE section per page, so three dense app screens cannot be built from it. Without `app-screen` the planner is confined to four marketing-shaped page archetypes and answers an app brief with a landing page ABOUT the app | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Four primitives added -- `Checkbox`, `Radio`, `Switch`, `Progress` | Having each app archetype hand-roll its own form controls from raw inputs | Contract 4.1's set is a MINIMUM and 4.2 reserves additions to the Design System Agent (same basis as 7.4's `Notice`). A form product cannot be built without choice controls, and every archetype that needs one needs the SAME one -- a checkbox hand-rolled per section is precisely the inconsistency primitives exist to prevent. `Switch` is deliberately distinct from `Checkbox` rather than a variant of it: it is for settings that apply immediately, which is what a properties inspector is made of, and it carries different ARIA (role=switch) | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Primitives now declare a BOUNDED DOM passthrough (aria-*, role, title, tabIndex, draggable, onClick/onDrag*/onFocus/onBlur) rather than either bespoke props or a full DOM spread | Adding `draggable`/`onDragStart` to Button and `onClick` to Card individually; or typing props as ComponentPropsWithoutRef | Found before spending, by checking the new templates against the primitive specs: `Button draggable`, `Card onClick` and `aria-invalid` on an input all appear in the app archetypes and NONE of them would have compiled -- a gate 1 typecheck failure on every retry, the exact shape of the 6.1 bugs that presented as "this archetype simply cannot pass". A full DOM prop spread was rejected because it collides with the value-shaped `onChange` signatures (DOM onChange takes an event, the primitives' takes a string), a type error the section author could not fix. One bounded rule fixes the whole class for every future template | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | `Icon`'s name union grew from 6 to 21 and is documented as CLOSED | Leaving 6 names and writing the app templates around them; or making `name` a free string | The app set needs a dozen field-type icons; with a closed 6-name union every one of them was a typecheck failure waiting to happen. A free string would have removed the failure by removing the guarantee -- the generated Icon would silently render nothing for an unknown name. The union is now explicitly closed in the spec, so every name in it must render, and the templates were mechanically checked against it | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The per-archetype prompt render test now parametrizes over EVERY dedicated template, and a new test forbids Jinja placeholders inside an [ARCHETYPE] teaching block | Leaving it at the hand-kept list of five 5.4 archetypes | The list was written at 5.4 and never grew, so 6.1's fourteen additions were never covered by it. The bug that cost 6.1 the most time was a placeholder leaking into an instructional example (`nodeId="{{section_slug}}.name-field"`), which the renderer substituted -- teaching the model a node id with the route prefix already missing, failing gate 4 identically on every retry, and only findable by reading the RENDERED prompt. That is now a mechanical test over all 27 templates rather than a lesson someone has to remember | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Page assembly builds its component name with a kebab-aware `page_component_name()` instead of `str.capitalize()` | Leaving it, since it had worked across every milestone | `"public-form".capitalize()` is `"Public-form"`, so assembly emitted `export default function Public-formPage()` -- a SYNTAX ERROR, not a naming wart. It survived milestones 1-7 because every route ever planned happened to be one word (home, shop, product, about, support); the first acceptance run to plan a two-word route hit it immediately. Route slugs are kebab-case by contract, so a hyphen is the general case rather than an edge one. Fixed in BOTH assemblers (the multi-section fan-out one and the single-section skeleton one) -- fixing only the one the failure surfaced in would have left the bug live on the other path | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | A page worker now EXITS NON-ZERO when its own assembled page fails its route-scoped gates | Leaving the worker to report the result and letting the project-wide gate run at the end catch it, as it did | `assemble_page` has always run route-scoped gates on the page it just wrote and returned the verdict; nothing ever read it. So the broken page above produced three workers all reporting `exit=0` and a run that failed later as an anonymous project-wide gate error -- the failure was caught, but attributed to nowhere. Making the worker fail on its own route is the entire point of scoping gates to a route (7.2). This does not weaken bounded retries: a section that exhausts them assembles a `FailedSectionPlaceholder`, which is valid code that passes gates, so the page still ships degraded per 5.4 | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Both defects above were found by the first END-TO-END RUN, not by any test, and both are in DETERMINISTIC code rather than model output | -- | Worth recording as evidence for what the acceptance runner is for. Every gate except gate 1 passed; the model output was fine; the bug was in the spine. It was caught only by gate 1's typecheck -- the half of "imports resolve; build passes" that 6.4 added after finding it had never been implemented -- which is the second time that specific addition has paid for itself | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Section generation's `max_tokens` raised from the 8192 default to `SECTION_MAX_TOKENS = 16000` | Making the data-grid archetype ask for less (fewer mock rows/columns), or splitting it into two sections | A section's structured output is the component, its mock data, one manifest proposal PER NODE, and its sectionMeta -- and the proposals come last, so a truncated response loses exactly what validation requires. The first app run's data-grid emitted a 7.5 KB component, spent the entire budget on files, and returned with `manifestProposals` and `sectionMeta` missing. Unrecoverable by retry: three attempts failed identically, never reached a gate, and the page shipped a FailedSectionPlaceholder. Shrinking the archetype would have hidden the limit rather than fixing it, and the next dense section would hit it again. Output tokens bill as emitted, so headroom costs nothing on sections that never approach it | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The acceptance summary now reports `degraded_sections` -- planned sections with no registered manifest node | Leaving the summary as routes + timings + cost, since a placeholder page is CORRECT behaviour per pipeline 5.4 | Degrading is correct; reporting plain success while doing it is not. The run that surfaced this exited 0, exported cleanly, and reported success with three routes -- while the submissions page shipped a placeholder where its data grid, the entire point of that screen, should have been. Nothing in the summary said so, and the only trace was three `section.generated` events with no matching `section.validated`. A run can be simultaneously successful and missing the thing it was asked for, and the summary has to be able to say both | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | `add_section` invoked the Kitaru flow DIRECTLY instead of `.run(...).wait()`; fixed, with a stub-based regression test | Leaving it to be caught by the next live run | Kitaru rejects a direct flow call outright (KitaruUsageError) because calling the function bypasses the execution that gives it checkpoints -- the very checkpoints regeneration later replays. It was the only such call site among nine in the orchestrator. Nothing caught it because mock mode short-circuits in TypeScript and never reaches this function, and the existing tests covered only the pure helpers around it (slugify, unique_section_slug, append_to_index, append_to_siteplan). This is precisely the gap the 7.6 decision row recorded as "not verified with a live model run", and it cost one live run to surface. The new test stubs a flow object reproducing Kitaru's real contract (`__call__` raises, `.run()` returns a handle), so a direct call now fails in CI rather than in a paid run | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | **7.9 and 7.6 are now both verified LIVE**, against generated content rather than the fixture | -- | 7.9: overrides seeded across style/text/visibility/sectionOrder on a real generated route survived a live section regen AND a live page regen, with zero orphans -- checked by "no override was lost while its node survived" rather than "zero orphans", which would have passed vacuously. 7.6: a `data-grid` was added live to an existing page in 84s, first attempt, landing appended in source with the FailedSectionPlaceholder holding its slot, and the export then compiled the reorder so the placeholder stayed in slot 2 while the sections moved around it | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | 7.5's strict section order was confirmed on live content, by observing it FAIL first | Only ever testing the success path | Exporting after the live add, with the order override still naming the two pre-existing sections, failed with `sectionOrder for route "submissions" omits "submissions.data-grid"`. That is the guard doing exactly the job it was designed for on real content: without it the section that had just been generated and paid for would have silently vanished from the export, and the omission would have looked like a reorder rather than a deletion | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | Page assembly gained PAGE LAYOUTS keyed by page archetype; `app-screen` wraps its sections in a flex row | Leaving assembly layout-agnostic and letting each section position itself; or adding a per-section `pane` field to the plan | Found by reviewing the first app-screen output: every screen rendered its panes STACKED VERTICALLY. The sections were right -- the palette carried `w-[18rem] shrink-0`, the canvas `flex-1`, the inspector `w-[20rem]` -- but those mean nothing unless something puts them in a row, and `assemble_page_index_source` always emitted a flat fragment. That is correct for a marketing page, where sections genuinely do stack down the screen, and wrong for an app screen. A section cannot fix this itself: it has no knowledge of its siblings. A per-section `pane` field was rejected as a schema change for something the `app-screen` prior already states (chrome first, then the panes) | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The layout is `flex-wrap` on ONE container, not a nested pane row | A nested row: chrome on top, then a `<div className="flex flex-1">` holding the panes -- the obvious markup | Nesting arranges the panes just as well and would break BOTH reorder implementations, which each operate on the section elements' common parent: the exporter rewrites that parent's children, and the shim moves real DOM nodes within it. With nesting, sections stop being siblings and reorder either fails or silently reorders one pane's contents. `flex-wrap` keeps every section a direct sibling: a full-width chrome section takes a whole line to itself and the panes fall onto the next line together, with no extra markup and no change to how reorder addresses them | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The exporter's reorder now finds the sections' real PARENT (anchored on the `nodeId` ATTRIBUTE) instead of assuming a JSX fragment, and rebuilds that parent with its own tags | Special-casing the app-screen wrapper | "The fragment" was only ever true because every page happened to return one. Now that a page archetype can wrap its sections, locating the container by finding a section and taking its parent is both more general and a match for what the shim already does at runtime -- so preview and export agree on which element they are reordering. Two traps: anchoring on "a node whose text contains the id" matches the OUTERMOST element first, because `getDescendants()` is document order, so the anchor is the `nodeId` attribute itself; and rebuilding as a bare `<>` would reorder correctly while silently deleting the layout classNames that position the panes | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | `app-shell` templates must put `w-full` on their root (template 1.0.1) | Forcing it from the layout wrapper with a positional selector like `[&>*:first-child]:w-full` | The positional version is more robust against a section forgetting, but it hardcodes "the first child is chrome" into the layout, which breaks the moment a screen has no chrome section or has two. How wide a section is remains the section's own business; the template now states the requirement, gives the reason (an app screen's wrapper is a flex row, so the chrome must claim a whole line) and lists omitting it as a failure mode | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The unexplained `builder.overrides.json` was NOT a defect: the EDITOR wrote it, exactly as the ownership map requires | Investigating further, or treating it as an unexplained write to a protected path | Evidence, not inference: `updatedAt` is a real millisecond ISO timestamp (`2026-08-02T13:53:21.426Z`) matching the file mtime, which is `new Date().toISOString()` from the editor's `toOverrideFile()` -- not the harness's fixed STAMP. The values are token refs of exactly the shape the inspector's colour swatches emit, plus one visibility toggle. And `form-public.overrides.json` was written EMPTY with the same timestamp, which is the tell: the editor's `writeOverrides` PUTs every route's file on every save. Recorded because the concern was raised in the open and should be closed in the open | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | `prepare_workspace_dir` now WIPES `overrides/` before scaffolding, the same way it wipes `src/pages/` | Deleting only the override files whose routes are not in the plan | Its own docstring already promised that "a single-route caller never inherits a stray fixture page"; overrides were simply missed, so the fixture's `about.overrides.json` and `support.overrides.json` survived into every generated project -- stale files naming routes that do not exist in that site, which then shipped in the export. Wiping wholesale matches what the pages directory already does, and for the same reason: the fixture is a multi-route test bed reused as a scaffold, so which files it happens to ship today is not something callers should have to know | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | The exporter now DROPS any `src/pages/<slug>/` with no entry in `routes.ts` | Special-casing the design-stage primitive gallery, which is the only known offender | The gallery is the case that surfaced it -- the Design System Agent writes it to `src/pages/home/index.tsx` so there is something to look at while no sections exist (5.2), and when the plan has no `home` route nothing cleans it up, so the handover ships an unreachable page importing every primitive. Filtering on routes.ts, the ground-truth route table, catches any orphan whatever produced it. It deliberately FAILS OPEN: if routes.ts cannot be read or yields no slugs, nothing is filtered, because shipping one extra directory is a blemish while deleting a real page on a regex miss is data loss | e2e |
| 2026-08-02 | List-item override compilation now RESOLVES a derived array back to the mock-data prop it came from | Forbidding derivation in the contract ("always map the prop directly; filter with `if (item.hidden) return null` inside the callback") | Found by an export failing on live content after a real editor edit: a generated `BuilderCanvas` wrote `const visibleFields = fields.filter((f) => f.hidden !== true)` and then `visibleFields.map(...)`, so the compiler went looking for a `visibleFields` array in mock data that only exports `fields`, and EVERY list-item override on such a section failed the export outright. The generated code is not wrong -- it is behaviourally identical to the `return null` form the templates teach, and is what a model reasonably writes. Tightening the contract would have made correct-looking code illegal and relied on every future generation remembering; resolving the derivation makes the exporter robust to a refactor that changes nothing about behaviour. The walk follows chained calls to their receiver and local consts to their initializer, stops at the first identifier that is not a local const (the destructured prop), is hop-bounded so a pathological chain terminates, and returns undefined on anything unrecognised so the caller skips the node rather than guessing at an array name | e2e |
| 2026-08-03 | Prompt-driven editing compiles an instruction to the SAME override operations the canvas produces, rather than regenerating | Regenerating the affected section per prompt | An override is deterministic, free, instantly reversible and preserves preview = handover; a regeneration is ~100x the cost and can rewrite content the user never mentioned. Structural requests are detected and routed to the existing regen/add-section flows behind a cost confirmation, so the capability is not lost -- only the silent spending is | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | The edit agent returns OPERATIONS; the editor applies them | The agent writing override files directly | The ownership map gives override files exactly one writer (contract section 2). Returning operations keeps that true and inherits validation, persistence, undo/redo and export unchanged -- the prompt becomes another way to author the same overrides, not a second kind of edit | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | The `style` operation takes a token path from an enum of the project's own tokens | Accepting a free string and validating afterwards | Makes a raw hex colour unrepresentable rather than merely rejected, which is the same guarantee gate 3 gives generated components. `styleExact` exists for a genuinely explicit "37px" and is counted off-scale in HANDOVER.md exactly as a canvas edit of that kind is | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | Validation is all-or-nothing per prompt | Applying the valid operations and reporting the rest | A compound instruction that half-lands ("made the CTA green but did not hide the badge") is worse than one that cleanly did not, and it keeps "one prompt = one undo entry" honest | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | Literal-node style/layout overrides now compile with a forced Tailwind `!important` suffix (`applyClassOverride`), matching the list-item path and the live shim's own `!important`-injected preview overrides | Leaving Tailwind's generated-stylesheet order to decide which same-property class wins | Found by the invariant suite's new prompt-driven case: an agent-authored `style` op set a Heading's `color` to an accent token, compiling correctly into its `className`, but Heading's own hardcoded base classes ALSO set a default text colour -- a second source for the same CSS property that lives in the primitive file, not the instance's own className string, so `mergeClassName`'s same-string conflict removal cannot reach it. Preview showed the override (the shim already forces `!important`); the built export's generated CSS happened to place Heading's default colour rule after the override's, so export silently kept the default -- a real preview/export mismatch, invisible until a `color` override on a primitive with a hardcoded default was ever exercised, which no canvas control (only background swatches) had done before this feature's prompt path | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | Measured per-edit cost is ~$0.0038 (one live call: 3361 input + 89 output tokens, Haiku 4.5, `edit` role, no escalation), reported as measured rather than reconciled to the spec's ~$0.001 estimate | Reporting $0.001 unchanged, or quietly re-deriving a smaller figure | $0.0038 is ~3.8x the spec's CACHED Haiku estimate but lands close to its own UNCACHED Haiku estimate (~$0.003) -- fair, since a single one-off measurement is necessarily uncached (`cache_creation_input_tokens` and `cache_read_input_tokens` both read 0 despite `cache_control` being set on the system block, consistent with this run's small, 37-node route falling under Anthropic's minimum cacheable-prompt length). Separately, `cost_for_run(run_id)` -- the read path the spec names -- does not surface this call at all: `edit_agent.py`'s `resolve_edit` reaches `record_usage`, which appends to the orchestrator-wide `runlog/usage.jsonl`, never to the per-run `<run_id>.jsonl` that `cost_for_run` reads, so a run's reported cost is provably unchanged before and after an edit call (confirmed: the per-run log's line count and last timestamp did not move). The real number above comes from that global usage log instead. Recorded rather than silently patched, since wiring `edit_agent.py` into the per-run log is outside this task's file list | edit-1 |
| 2026-08-03 | The editor reads the `/__edit-prompt` response through one pure function (`interpretEditResult`) that treats JSON `null` and an absent key as the same thing | Changing the two `!== undefined` comparisons in `App.tsx` to `!= null` | The bug: the Python agent's `_normalize` emits an explicit `None` for an absent `clarify`/`structural`, which crosses the wire as `null`, so `result.structural !== undefined` was TRUE on every response and `result.structural.kind` threw -- every real prompt failed, 100% of the time. Nothing caught it because the two producers disagree in shape and only one was ever tested: `mockEditOperations` OMITS those keys, and every automated test runs in mock mode. Comparing against `null` would have fixed the symptom while leaving the seam untested and the next producer free to disagree again; a named function at the boundary is testable directly, so the real payload shape (explicit nulls, taken verbatim from `_normalize`) is now a unit test rather than something only a live model call could exercise | edit-review |
| 2026-08-03 | Every orchestrator spawn from the preview server is shell-FREE (`runProcess`, one helper, no `shell: true`) | Quoting the arguments for `cmd.exe` before passing them through a shell | `shell: true` makes Node concatenate argv into one unquoted command string (its own DEP0190 deprecation says exactly this), so `--instruction "make the headline shorter"` reached argparse as five arguments: it exited 2, printed no result line, and `/__regen`, `/__regen-page`, `/__add-section` and `/__edit-prompt` could not work AT ALL outside mock mode. It was also a shell-injection surface fed straight from a text box -- demonstrated live: an instruction containing `&` ran the following word as a separate command. Quoting would have to be correct for cmd.exe, PowerShell and sh, and would still leave the injection surface open; spawning the executable directly has no quoting layer to get wrong. Verified empirically on Windows, where the concern was that a bare `uv` needs a shell to resolve: it does not (libuv searches PATH and PATHEXT and finds `uv.exe`), and with the shell gone argparse accepted a multi-word instruction containing a quote and an `&`, failing later at the intended point (a missing project) instead of at argument parsing | edit-review |
| 2026-08-03 | `mockEditOperations` now returns explicit `clarify: null` / `structural: null` when absent, instead of omitting the keys, mirroring `edit_agent.py`'s `_normalize` exactly | Leaving the mock's omitted-key shape, since `interpretEditResult` already treats null and absent alike | A mock that differs in WIRE SHAPE from the real producer is worse than no mock: every automated test ran the mock's omitted-key shape, so 100% of real prompts failed (the earlier `!== undefined` bug this session already fixed) while the suite stayed green throughout. Matching the real shape here is what makes the 99 editor e2e tests actually exercise the shape production sends, at zero model spend | edit-review |
| 2026-08-04 | `POST /api/login` requires `Content-Type: application/json` (media type parsed off the `;`, matched case-insensitively, exact equality after trim/lowercase) and 400s otherwise, before the body is read | Relying on `SameSite=Lax` alone, which the spec already mandates on the session cookie | `SameSite=Lax` guards requests that SEND the session cookie; login SETS one, so it is not covered. A cross-site HTML `<form>` posting `text/plain` is the standard login-CSRF bypass and needs no preflight. Impact today is nil -- nothing is gated yet -- but slice 3 stores users' Anthropic API keys, and then the attack is: log the victim's browser into the ATTACKER's account, wait for them to paste their key into what looks like their own settings, then sign in and take it. A form can only send three content types, none of them `application/json`, and a cross-origin `fetch` with that type triggers a CORS preflight that fails. Exact equality, not `startsWith`/`includes`, so `application/json-patch+json` cannot slip through. The change is invisible in tests unless the shared `call()` helper sends the header by default, which is why every pre-existing login test was re-checked for MEANING, not just for green | slice-2 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `openDatabase` sets `PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000`, and the guarantee is tested with two real connections (a worker thread holding `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, released by its own timer) rather than by reading the pragma back | Leaving the SQLite default of 0 and treating `database is locked` as a caller concern | WAL allows many readers but still one writer, and the CLI and the server are two PROCESSES on one file. At timeout 0 the loser throws immediately instead of retrying, which means (a) an emergency `disable` prints an error and exits 1 with the revocation NOT applied -- the exact path server-side sessions exist to make reliable -- and (b) `createSession` throwing inside login surfaces as a 500 on CORRECT credentials. Structurally invisible to every per-task review, because every test in the package used a single connection; it took a whole-branch reviewer opening two handles to see it. The pragma readback alone would not have caught a regression, since it proves the setting exists rather than that a writer waits and then succeeds | slice-2 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | The router builds `new URL()` inside its own guard and answers a malformed `Host` with 400 (no fallback to a default host); `serve.ts`'s `unhandledRejection`/`uncaughtException` handlers arm INSIDE the `listen` callback, never at module scope, and a bind failure exits 1 | Constructing the URL with a `localhost` fallback; registering the process handlers at the top of the script where they are easiest to see | The URL was built before the try, so a malformed Host threw a `TypeError` that rejected the listener promise -- `node:http` installs no handler for that and Node terminated the PROCESS. On a single-VM, single-process deployment, one unauthenticated 60-byte request ended every user's session until a human restarted; 15 of 20 probed Host values did it. A `localhost` fallback would answer a malformed request as if it were addressed to us, which is worse than rejecting it. The handlers then had to move: at module scope they swallowed every STARTUP failure too, so an unopenable `--db` or a taken port logged "server continues" and exited 0 while serving nothing -- and a supervisor reads exit 0 as "do not restart". Armed after `listen`, the trade flips correctly: startup stays loudly fatal, runtime stays resilient | slice-2 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | One shared `flag()` for every operator script, which treats a `--`-prefixed token as "no value supplied", plus a minimal email check on `user create` (one `@`, non-empty and whitespace-free either side) | Per-command argument validation, or an RFC 5322-grade email validator | `flag()` returned whatever token followed `--name`, including the next flag's NAME. `set-cap`/`disable`/`enable` were saved by their own existence checks, but `create` had neither a format check nor a record to fail against, so `create --email --usd 50` silently made a user whose email was the literal string `--usd`, dropped the 50, and exited 0 -- an account nobody can log into, in the one command that is the sole way an account exists at all. A third, unfixed copy of the same parser then turned up in `serve.ts`, where `--db --port 4000` served an EMPTY identity database: every correct login returned the uniform 401, indistinguishable from a wrong password, so the operator debugs credentials instead of the path. One implementation, one place left to get wrong. The email check is deliberately shallow -- it catches operator typos, it is not an address policy | slice-2 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `server/package.json` declares `"engines": { "node": ">=22.13" }`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml` was left on Node 22 and the mismatch with the plan's "Node 24" was flagged to the human rather than edited | Raising CI to Node 24 to match the plan text, or adding an `.nvmrc` | `node:sqlite` -- which the whole identity store depends on -- was unflagged only in Node 22.13.0, and nothing in the repo pinned a floor, so a VM provisioned with 22.5-22.12 would fail at boot with `ERR_UNKNOWN_BUILTIN_MODULE`. `engines` states the real requirement and is scoped to the package that has it. Changing CI's Node version affects every package, not just this one, and the plan-vs-CI disagreement is a doc/config conflict, which the standing rule says to escalate rather than resolve by editing | slice-2 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `serve.ts` deletes `WEBGEN_MASTER_KEY` from `process.env` immediately after `loadMasterKey()` returns the Buffer | Relying on `buildAgentEnv`'s per-spawn `delete` of the variable from the environment copy it builds | `buildAgentEnv` protects only spawns that opt in, and the dangerous spawns do not: `compiler/src/regen-api.ts:238` (`uv run python`), `exporter.ts:1210` (the generated project's OWN `vite build`) and `gates.ts:227` (`tsc`) all pass no `env` option, so they inherit the parent's environment wholesale -- verified by reading all three. Slice 4 mounts `/__export` on this root, so a user exporting would run model-generated config and plugin code -- untrusted input, per the spec's accepted risk 1 -- with the master key in its environment, and one `process.env` read would decrypt EVERY user's stored key. That is precisely the "one escape would be total" outcome `agent-env.ts` exists to prevent, and it was invisible because `buildAgentEnv` has no caller yet. The apparent conflict with "process.env is never mutated" is not one: that rule exists to keep a USER's key out of the parent environment, and this deletion removes a secret rather than adding one. Every read site was enumerated to confirm nothing calls `loadMasterKey()` a second time and would now throw | slice-3 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `resolveApiKey` refuses a disabled user, and refuses a PASTED key for one too, with a distinct `UnknownUserError` for a user id with no row | Checking `disabled_at` only on the stored-key path, since the pasted path never touches the database | `disable` revokes sessions, which closes the HTTP surface -- but that is a different guarantee. Decision 13 requires work in flight to survive disconnect, so slice 4 resolves keys from a project's `owner_id` rather than from a live session; without this check an offboarded or suspected-compromised user's key keeps being spent and the operator gets no signal that `disable` was insufficient. Honouring a pasted key would let a disabled account keep spending under a different credential, which defeats the point of `disable` in exactly the suspected-compromise case it exists for. The unknown/disabled split matters because a bad `owner_id` is a realistic slice-4 bug, and "this account is disabled" sends an operator looking in the wrong place when there is no account at all -- both fail closed, only the message differs | slice-3 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `getApiKeyFingerprint` takes no master-key parameter at all; only `getApiKeyPlaintext` -- named so nobody reaches for it by accident -- returns the credential | One accessor with a flag or an optional master key, returning either the fingerprint or the plaintext | A signature without the key makes a display-only caller STRUCTURALLY incapable of decrypting, which is a stronger guarantee than a convention a future handler can forget. The settings screen only ever needs the last four characters (spec, BYOK requirement 3), and a hijacked session must not be able to harvest the credential. Verified as effective: `getApiKeyPlaintext` has exactly one production caller (`resolveApiKey`), which has exactly one (`buildAgentEnv`), so there is no path by which a route decrypts a key for anyone other than the session's own user. The cost is a real one worth recording -- a fingerprint read cannot detect that a row has become undecryptable, so after a master-key rotation the UI reports a healthy key that cannot be used, which is why `getApiKeyPlaintext` now raises a typed `UndecryptableApiKeyError` rather than node's bare GCM message | slice-3 implementation |
| 2026-08-04 | The 16-byte AES-GCM authentication tag is appended to the `ciphertext` column rather than given a fourth column | A separate `tag` column alongside `ciphertext` and `nonce` | Keeps the table to exactly the columns the design names (`ciphertext, nonce, fingerprint, created_at`), and is the layout libsodium's secretbox already uses, so it reads as conventional rather than clever. The one hazard is arithmetic: `open` splits the last 16 bytes off, so the short-ciphertext guard must be a strict `<` -- an empty plaintext yields a ciphertext of EXACTLY 16 bytes and must still round-trip, which a `<=` guard would reject. That boundary is now a test, because it is the kind of off-by-one a later "fix" reintroduces | slice-3 implementation |
| 2026-08-04 | `createRequestListener` throws on a duplicate `(method, path)` at composition time, and route composition moved into a testable `buildRoutes` rather than living inline in `serve.ts` | Leaving `routes.find(...)`'s first-match-wins behaviour, and leaving the composition root untested | The route table IS the allowlist, so its integrity deserves an assertion rather than a silent shadowing: slice 4 adds ten project-scoped routes across multiple arrays, and a path registered in the wrong one would be masked by whichever spread came first, with no error anywhere. The composition root had zero automated coverage -- a dropped `...keyRoutes(...)` spread, or `loadMasterKey()` moved after `openDatabase`, passed all 157 tests, with the only evidence a manual PowerShell run that CI cannot repeat. `buildRoutes` is now tested for the exact `(method, path)` set, and four source-position tests pin the boot ordering that a previous bug on this branch already violated once (module-scope `uncaughtException` handlers swallowing startup failures into a silent exit 0) | slice-3 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-04 | `redactSecrets` ships deliberately INERT -- built, tested six ways, and called by nothing -- with its docstring saying so explicitly | Wiring it into a call site now so it is not dead code, or deleting it until needed | The spec requires that the key is never logged and that redaction happen at the boundary where the key is read. The first half already holds BY CONSTRUCTION, verified call-by-call: `console.*` is the only output sink in `server/`, and none of its ten call sites receives a key-derived value -- `setApiKey` binds ciphertext rather than plaintext, and the router deliberately logs nothing on a 500, so even a throw from the `PUT` handler with plaintext in scope reaches no sink. Inventing a call site to avoid dead code would add a log line that does not need to exist. Deleting it would lose the one thing it is for: slice 4 will log spawn errors and buffered subprocess stderr, which is exactly the message class nobody constructs and therefore cannot vet. The real risk was misplaced confidence -- the plan credited it with the guarantee -- so the docstring now states plainly that it is inert, that the guarantee currently rests on construction, and that slice 4 must call it | slice-3 whole-branch review |
| 2026-08-05 | Route paths may declare at most ONE `:name` segment; matching stays segment-exact (equal segment counts, empty segment rejected, malformed percent-escape treated as a non-match), and literals are matched before parameterised routes in a separate pass | Prefix matching, or a regex router; or sorting the table so literals happen to come first | `/__overrides/<slug>` and `/api/projects/<id>` cannot be matched by the exact equality that makes the route table an allowlist, so something had to give -- but not the allowlist. A parameter that could span a slash would mean registering one route silently exposes every path beneath it, INCLUDING paths with their own intended authorization rule, which is the whole property being protected. Two passes rather than sorting, so a literal collection endpoint cannot be shadowed by an id lookup registered before it, regardless of array order. Three details are load-bearing and each is pinned by a test: an empty segment is not a value (else a trailing slash looks up row "" after a database read on unvalidated input); decoding happens AFTER the split, so a percent-encoded slash cannot smuggle an extra segment past the matcher; and `decodeURIComponent` THROWS on a malformed escape -- unguarded, that rejected the async listener's promise, which node:http never awaits, so the request got no response at all and hung to timeout. The dedupe key normalises param segments, because before this change string equality WAS pattern equality and the duplicate guard was complete; afterwards `/a/:x` and `/a/:y` would not have been flagged and the second would be silently unreachable | slice-4a implementation + review |
| 2026-08-05 | A foreign project and a nonexistent one return byte-identical responses (404, one shared constant, one `sendJson` call, both failure modes collapsed into a single `if`), and `requireProject` composes OVER `requireSession` | Returning 403 for a project that exists but belongs to someone else, which is the more literally accurate status | 403-vs-404 turns every project-scoped endpoint into an enumeration oracle: an attacker learns which project ids exist from the difference, without ever being authorized for one. Collapsing both into one branch is deliberate -- with two branches a future caller can report them differently by accident, and a test asserting equal STATUS would still pass while the bodies diverged, so the test compares bodies too. Composing over `requireSession` rather than repeating it means an unauthenticated request never triggers a project lookup, which is a small oracle of its own, and keeps authentication and authorization one decision each rather than two per handler | slice-4a implementation |
| 2026-08-05 | A project's `directory` is stored NORMALISED and relative to the projects root; it is validated on the way in and re-checked by `resolveProjectDirectory` on the way out, and the two guards are tested to agree | Validating once at the boundary and trusting the row thereafter; or storing an absolute path | The column is a filesystem path arriving FROM the database, and slices 4c and 5 hand it to a process spawn -- so the re-check exists precisely for a row written by an earlier schema version, which by definition was never validated going in. Two bugs found here are worth recording because both were invisible in a single-platform, single-writer world. First, the guards disagreed: `resolveProjectDirectory` rejected any relative path merely BEGINNING with two dots, so a legitimate directory named `..foo` was accepted at creation and then permanently rejected by the one function that exists to resolve it. Second, validation ran on the normalised form while the INSERT stored the raw argument, so `run-a`, `./run-a` and `a/../run-a` were three distinct rows resolving to one real path -- which silently breaks `UNIQUE(directory)` as "one owner per directory" (two preview servers on one tree) and breaks adoption's idempotency, since `findProjectByDirectory` matches an exact spelling. Unreachable while adoption was the only writer and always passed a bare dirent name; slice 5 is the writer that would have tripped it | slice-4a review |
| 2026-08-05 | The project-scoped endpoint registry is a PARTITION of the live route table -- every method-and-path `buildRoutes` returns must appear in exactly one of project-scoped, session-only, or unauthenticated -- with the not-yet-mounted compiler endpoints excluded by an explicit predicate whose excluded set is itself pinned by a test | The first implementation: a test comparing the registry against a hardcoded list written inside the same test file | The spec requires that adding an endpoint without a rule FAILS a test. The hardcoded-list version could not do that in the direction that matters: it failed only when an entry was REMOVED from the registry, and nothing in production consumed the registry at all. The concrete hole it left -- a developer adds a project-scoped route to the composition, updates the expected-routes test to keep it green, wraps it in `requireSession` instead of `requireProject`, and ships an endpoint any logged-in user can point at any project id, with every test passing. Making it a partition of the real table is what closes that. The reason the bidirectional test was not written first is itself informative: login and logout were in NEITHER list, so the two lists were not a partition of anything, and adding a third category was the missing precondition | slice-4a review |
| 2026-08-05 | First-boot adoption of existing generated directories is idempotent, never reassigns an owner, never creates a user, and distinguishes "projects root missing or unreadable" from "projects root empty" | Adopting once behind a flag; or letting an unresolved bootstrap email create the bootstrap account | Adoption runs on EVERY boot, so idempotency has to be structural rather than a caller's discipline -- `directory` is UNIQUE and an existing row is SKIPPED rather than updated, which is what stops a later boot with a different bootstrap email from silently moving somebody's projects. It must not create a user because no code path outside the operator CLI may bring one into existence; invite-only is structural, so an unresolved email is a warning and a skip. The missing-versus-empty distinction was a real defect: `readdirSync`'s bare catch swallowed ENOENT and the default resolved to a nonexistent directory under the documented start command, so a total no-op logged as "0 adopted, 0 already known" -- a success-shaped line -- while the acceptance runs the spec's retention requirement exists to preserve sat unadopted | slice-4a implementation + review |
| 2026-08-05 | Refusing billable work past the spend cap answers **402 Payment Required**, not 429, and the body carries `capUsd`, `spentUsd` and `resetAt` as fields as well as inside the sentence | 429 Too Many Requests, which is the more familiar "you are being limited" status | 429 has an established client contract: every HTTP library, proxy and SDK retries it with backoff. That behaviour is precisely wrong here, because retrying cannot help until the window rolls — it just burns requests and makes the user think the service is broken. 402 says what actually happened. The numbers appear twice on purpose: the spec requires the error to state the cap, the spend and the reset time, and a UI that has to regex a prose sentence to render a countdown will get it wrong, while a sentence built only from fields cannot be read by a human in a log | slice-4b implementation |
| 2026-08-05 | A cap that is non-positive, NaN **or infinite** refuses all work and reports no reset time; `Infinity` is the case that makes the guard load-bearing rather than decorative | Letting the ordinary `spent < cap` comparison handle every unusual cap value | The plan specified a perturbation to prove this guard mattered — set the cap to 0, delete the guard, watch the test fail — and the implementer reported honestly that the test still PASSED. That was correct: with a zero, negative or NaN cap the comparison already refuses (`0 < 0` is false, and every comparison against NaN is false) and the reset walk already returns null on an empty event list, so the guard changes nothing. Exactly one input distinguishes it: `Infinity`, where `spent < Infinity` is always true, so removing the guard turns one corrupt row into authorisation for unlimited spend on someone else's API key. `set-cap` validates `Number.isFinite`, so Infinity can only arrive from a corrupted row — and a corrupted cap must fail closed. The test now constructs the `User` object directly rather than writing Infinity through SQLite, since `checkSpendCap` takes a `User` and never reads the row | slice-4b implementation + review |
| 2026-08-05 | The reset time is computed **exactly** — in-window events are dropped oldest-first until the remainder is under the cap, and the answer is that event's expiry plus one millisecond — rather than estimated as "oldest event + 24h" | Reporting the oldest in-window event's expiry, or simply "try again in 24 hours" | Dropping the oldest event is only correct when dropping ONE is enough; with $2 + $2 + $9 against a $10 cap, the first expiry still leaves $11 and the user is refused again on the retry, which is worse than no number at all. The walk is exact because spend can only ever DECREASE as the window slides — every recorded event is already in the past — so the future is fully determined by rows already stored. The `+ 1` is not a fudge: `spendSince` uses `at >= since`, so at the instant `event.at + 24h` the boundary still includes that event. The property is pinned by asserting against the policy itself — `checkSpendCap(resetAt).allowed` is true while `checkSpendCap(resetAt - 1).allowed` is false — which cannot pass vacuously and is what caught the off-by-one | slice-4b implementation |
| 2026-08-05 | A future-dated usage timestamp is **clamped to the ingest time** (`Math.min(parsedAt, now)`) at the one place rows enter; the window query was deliberately NOT changed to add `at <= now` | Adding an upper bound to `spendSince`/`eventsSince`, which is where the problem surfaces | The whole-branch review found that the reset walk's exactness rests on an invariant nothing enforced: every stored event is in the past. One future-dated row — host clock skew, a mangled but parseable date — would sit in the trailing-24h window until its own timestamp passed, blocking the user until then and making the computed reset instant a lie. The fix had to go at ingest rather than in the query, because `at <= now` in the query would HIDE that spend instead of correcting it, and hiding spend is the dangerous direction: the user would be billed for a call the cap never counted. Related: a negative `cost_usd` is likewise treated as unpriced rather than trusted, since a negative number SUBTRACTS from the window and under-counts. The invariant remains conventional rather than structural — `recordUsageEvent` is exported and applies no clamp — which is recorded here so the next author knows it exists | slice-4b review |
| 2026-08-05 | `billable` is a **required** field on every registry entry, not an optional one, and the rate card stays in Python (`pricing.py`) with each usage row carrying its own `cost_usd` | An optional field defaulting to false; a separate BILLABLE list; a second copy of Anthropic's rates in TypeScript | A required field makes an undeclared new endpoint a COMPILE error rather than something a reviewer has to notice — the type system catches it before the code runs, which no test can do. The rates stay in one language for the same reason two copies of anything about money are dangerous: they drift the moment a price changes and then disagree about a real user's real bill. Pricing at the point of record means the server sums a number it was handed. `cost_usd` is NULL, never 0.0, for a model with no published rate (the `ORCH_MODEL_PROVIDER=gemini` escape hatch), because a zero reads as "this call was free"; the count of such events is reported alongside the total so all three surfaces — the 402 body, the operator CLI and `/api/me` — can say the figure is a floor rather than presenting an uncertain number as a confident one | slice-4b implementation + review |
| 2026-08-05 | `ingestUsageLog` must never throw, and reports the SIZE of any loss: `skipped` is computed as `total - ingested` from an outer backstop, with a separate `unreadable` flag for a file that could not be read at all | Letting exceptions propagate; or a bare outer catch returning zeroes | The caller (4c) runs this in a `finally` after a subprocess spawn that may itself have failed, so a throw here loses the billing AND masks the original error. Three defects were found in sequence, each invisible to the review before it. First, the plan guarded a dangling `projectId` with a comment explaining that a foreign-key violation "would throw on the first insert and discard the whole run's billing" — and never applied that reasoning to `userId`, which is `NOT NULL REFERENCES user(id)` and constant for the whole file, so a stale one lost 100% of a run; the reviewer reproduced it rather than arguing it. Second, two `db.prepare().get()` reads stayed unguarded while the docstring claimed the function never throws. Third — and this is the shape worth remembering — the outer catch returned `{ingested: 0, skipped: 0}`, which is byte-identical to the legitimate result for a run that made no model calls, so an entire run's spend vanished into a success-shaped value while the docstring claimed "a loss is visible in `skipped`, never silent." Counting usable lines BEFORE any fallible operation is what makes that claim true. **Ingest is deliberately not idempotent**: ingesting the same file twice bills twice, so 4c must ingest exactly once from a per-invocation path and delete the file — the safe-looking retry is the one that doubles a bill | slice-4b review |
| 2026-08-05 | The contract between the orchestrator's usage-log writer and the hosted server's reader is pinned by a machine-generated golden file (`fixtures/usage-log-contract.jsonl`), asserted from both languages | Trusting that two components written from separate task briefs agree; or a cross-language test that shells out from vitest into uv | The two halves were written by different implementers from different briefs and, until the whole-branch review, nothing anywhere proved a file written by `record_usage` was readable by `ingestUsageLog`. They did agree — including that Python's `isoformat()` emits six fractional digits and a `+00:00` offset, neither of which is in the ECMAScript Date Time String grammar, which V8 nonetheless parses by truncating to milliseconds — but nothing protected that. A shape change (`timestamp` renamed, usage nested under a `usage` key as `pricing.run_cost` expects) would have turned 100% of a run's billing into `skipped` with both suites still green. A golden file keeps the two suites independent — neither package gains a dependency on the other's toolchain — while giving them one artifact that fails if either side drifts. It is pinned to LF in `.gitattributes`, since `core.autocrlf` would otherwise make the bytes of a byte-level contract depend on which OS cloned the repo | slice-4b review |
| 2026-08-05 | The registry's "a billable endpoint must be gated" tripwire is keyed on the **live composed route table**, and is named for what it can observe (mountedness, not gatedness) | The first implementation: filtering on `isUnmountedCompilerEndpoint(path)`, a `path.startsWith("/__")` predicate | This reintroduced, in a new place, the exact defect a previous slice already fixed once and recorded here — a test that compares the registry against something inside itself and therefore fails nothing. A pure function of the path string cannot notice a route being mounted, so the assertion would have stayed green while 4c mounted a billable endpoint with no budget gate at all; the reviewer proved it by simulating exactly that. Two further points are deliberate. The test consults `buildRoutes` so it fails the moment anything billable is mounted, gated or not — coarse on purpose, because a `Route` is `{method, path, handler}` and nothing records whether a handler is wrapped, so no test at this level can distinguish the two. And it is named for mountedness rather than for gate presence, because a test name that claims a check it cannot perform is the same failure as a comment claiming a protection the code does not provide | slice-4b review |
| 2026-08-05 | Vite does **not** honour port 0, so any caller needing a dynamic preview port must probe for one in the parent and pass it concretely; `strictPort: true` must not be removed, because it is what makes losing the probe's check-then-use race safe | Asking Vite for port 0 and letting the OS assign an ephemeral port, which is what slice 4c-1's plan originally specified | Verified empirically twice, once by the implementer and once independently: `--port 0` prints `PREVIEW_READY {"port":5173,...}`. Vite treats `0` as "no port configured" and falls back to its own default, so two children both asking for 0 collide. This matters beyond one plan because the obvious reaction to a port collision is to relax `strictPort` — and `strictPort` is precisely the mechanism that turns a lost probe race into a loud child exit a parent can retry, instead of a silent rebind onto some other port that the parent then proxies to nobody. A second defect shared the same root and is the more general lesson: the test written to prove port 0 worked asserted only `port !== 5273` (the CLI's own default), and Vite's fallback is 5173, so it passed green under the title "binds an OS-assigned port when asked for port 0". An assertion that excludes one wrong value is not an assertion that requires the right behaviour — and a title is not a test. The replacement pins Vite's literal fallback deliberately: if a future Vite bump reddens it, that is the tripwire working, and the premise should be re-examined rather than the number updated | slice-4c-1 implementation + review |
| 2026-08-05 | A preview is always a **child process**; `startPreviewServer` is never called in the hosted server's own process | Running the pool in-process — far simpler: no port probing, no lifecycle, no reverse proxy, no WebSocket forwarding | The server process holds the master key that decrypts **every** user's stored API key, and `startPreviewServer` loads the project's own `vite.config.ts` by design, so the project's plugins resolve from the project's own `node_modules`. That config is unvalidated input sitting in a directory whose other contents are model-generated. Importing it in-process would put one `process.env` read between untrusted code and every user's key. The spec's accepted risk 1 concedes that same-origin preview lets generated code reach the editor's DOM — a browser-side risk it priced in; loading unvalidated config into the key-holding process is a different and much larger one that nothing in the spec accepts. The subprocess boundary is also what makes "reaped, max 6" meaningful at all: an in-process server cannot be killed without taking the API host with it | slice-4c-1 implementation |

The host's key must go because if a keyless user's child inherited it, generation would still *work* — and the operator would pay, for a user whose `usage_event` rows record them as having spent it. Nothing would surface the transfer: ingestion attributes spend to the requesting user regardless of whose key paid, so the bill and the accounting would disagree silently and permanently. Absent is correct; inherited is a silent transfer. Previewing stays free because `MissingApiKeyError` is caught and falls back to the scrubbed environment, while `UnknownUserError` and `DisabledUserError` propagate — and that owner check runs on the **reuse** path too, not just on a fresh spawn, so a preview already running stops being reachable the moment its owner is disabled rather than depending on nothing else holding a warm process | slice-4c-1 implementation + review |

The child is spawned with `--base /preview/<projectId>/`, and Vite given a non-root base expects every request to arrive WITH that prefix — it uses it both to write asset URLs into the HTML and to route the request internally. A stripped request gets a 302 back to the very prefix just removed, and proxying that redirect through unexamined is an infinite loop from the client's side, because the "corrected" URL is the one it already sent. The query string is equally load-bearing and equally easy to lose: Vite's HMR handshake carries a token in it, so a version that rebuilt the path from decoded segments would break HMR while leaving every test green — which is why a test now pins it. `ctx.params["*"]` still exists, because the wildcard is what lets the route match every path under the prefix at all; it is simply not used to build the forwarded path | slice-4c-1 implementation |

First, Vite does not honour port 0 — it treats `0` as "no port configured" and falls back to its own default, so every child asking for 0 collides (verified empirically twice). Second, and more important: that line arrives on the **stdout of a child running unvalidated project code**, which can print its own well-formed `PREVIEW_READY {"port":9999}` before the CLI does. Believing it would hand untrusted input a redirect primitive — the pool would proxy every request for that project to whatever port it named. Since the parent chose the port and `strictPort` guarantees the child either bound exactly it or died loudly, the announced value is redundant, so reading it is pure downside. | 2026-08-06 | Residual 1's fix: the proxy now holds an aborted exchange open — on a client disconnect, `proxyHttp` no longer destroys the upstream request; it lets the child run to completion and discards the bytes once nobody is listening, only then settling with `completed: true` | Keeping the early-settle-and-destroy behaviour and only fixing `pool.release`/`releaseBillableSlot` to free on a separate, later signal | This is a genuine, observable behaviour change worth its own row: a request whose CLIENT has gone can now hold a loopback TCP connection to the preview child open for up to the full 15 minutes of `PREVIEW_PROXY_TIMEOUT_MS`, where before it was torn down within one event-loop tick of the abort. The alternative (destroy on abort, but decouple `release`/`after()` from the destruction) does not exist as a real option: the compiler child has no abort listener of its own — `uv run python` runs a regeneration or export to completion regardless of whether the HTTP response is still wanted — so destroying the CONNECTION never stopped the WORK; it only made this proxy stop watching it, which is exactly what let `release`/`releaseBillableSlot` free early. A client-facing `res` that has already left is drained (`resume()`), never written to, so no bytes are wasted on a socket nobody reads; only the loopback connection to the child, and this promise's own resolution, wait for genuine completion. A genuine upstream timeout is UNCHANGED: 15 minutes in, `proxyHttp` still destroys the upstream, answers 504 if possible, and settles with `completed: false` — that loss stays accepted, per the pre-existing `PREVIEW_PROXY_TIMEOUT_MS` comment | fix/proxy-residuals |
